Yeah… I guess it is, more than I’d like to admit. I’m still getting things done, mostly, but I’ve been falling behind on support tickets, and I’ll double-check really simple tasks over and over, which slows me down. A couple coworkers have had to help cover things, which I feel pretty bad about.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling really wound up most of the time. It’s probably not, like, dramatic or anything, but my body feels tense a lot—almost like I’m bracing for something bad to happen, even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax and enjoy dance night. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you think is causing you to feel this tension?\n\nPatient: I mean… there isn’t just one thing, I don’t think. It used to be more about my condo stuff, but now it kind of jumps around. Like I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m worried I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, and then it’s money—like how I’m supposed to save or move forward with my life.\n\nIt sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it’s like my brain keeps finding the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually notice the tension in your body when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always notice it right away, but when I’m worrying, my body feels kind of wound up—like I’m bracing for something bad to happen. Even if I’m just at my desk or trying to have a normal night out dancing, there’s this tension there. I don’t know if that’s the “right” way to describe it, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a wide range of things, or are they mostly focused on a few specific areas?\n\nPatient: It’s… more of a range, I think. It used to feel mostly tied to the condo, so that was easier to explain, but now it doesn’t really stay there. It’ll jump to, like, whether my cousin is upset with me, or if I’m going to make some mistake on a support call, or money—like how I’m supposed to save and move forward.\n\nI know those are kind of normal things to think about, probably, but it feels like my mind just keeps picking a new one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are bigger than the problems themselves?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like my problems are huge or anything, because they’re pretty normal things, I think. But my reaction to them feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike, it doesn’t just stay on the condo anymore. It jumps to, “Is my cousin upset with me?” or “What if I mess up a support call?” or “How am I ever going to save money or move forward?” And each one can feel really important in the moment, even if later I can see maybe it wasn’t as big as it felt.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do or enjoy the things you like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is, more than I’d like to admit. I’m still getting things done, mostly, but I’ve been falling behind on support tickets, and I’ll double-check really simple tasks over and over, which slows me down. A couple coworkers have had to help cover things, which I feel pretty bad about.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped some dance nights and mentoring sessions. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but honestly sometimes I feel too overwhelmed to even show up. I don’t know if that counts as affecting things, but… it probably does.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worries keep coming back to you over time, even after you've tried to deal with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I mean, I try to handle it, so it’s not like I’m ignoring things. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I sent the email, it’s done,” but then it still kind of comes back around.\n\nAnd then one little “what if” turns into, like, five more before I can really pull myself out of it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but… yeah, it does keep looping sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think so. I can usually start by telling myself something reasonable, like, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.” But it doesn’t always stick.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling really wound up most of the time. It’s probably not, like, dramatic or anything, but my body feels tense a lot—almost like I’m bracing for something bad to happen, even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax and enjoy dance night. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you think is causing you to feel this tension?\n\nPatient: I mean… there isn’t just one thing, I don’t think. It used to be more about my condo stuff, but now it kind of jumps around. Like I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m worried I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, and then it’s money—like how I’m supposed to save or move forward with my life.\n\nIt sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it’s like my brain keeps finding the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually notice the tension in your body when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always notice it right away, but when I’m worrying, my body feels kind of wound up—like I’m bracing for something bad to happen. Even if I’m just at my desk or trying to have a normal night out dancing, there’s this tension there. I don’t know if that’s the “right” way to describe it, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a wide range of things, or are they mostly focused on a few specific areas?\n\nPatient: It’s… more of a range, I think. It used to feel mostly tied to the condo, so that was easier to explain, but now it doesn’t really stay there. It’ll jump to, like, whether my cousin is upset with me, or if I’m going to make some mistake on a support call, or money—like how I’m supposed to save and move forward.\n\nI know those are kind of normal things to think about, probably, but it feels like my mind just keeps picking a new one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are bigger than the problems themselves?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like my problems are huge or anything, because they’re pretty normal things, I think. But my reaction to them feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike, it doesn’t just stay on the condo anymore. It jumps to, “Is my cousin upset with me?” or “What if I mess up a support call?” or “How am I ever going to save money or move forward?” And each one can feel really important in the moment, even if later I can see maybe it wasn’t as big as it felt.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do or enjoy the things you like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is, more than I’d like to admit. I’m still getting things done, mostly, but I’ve been falling behind on support tickets, and I’ll double-check really simple tasks over and over, which slows me down. A couple coworkers have had to help cover things, which I feel pretty bad about.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped some dance nights and mentoring sessions. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but honestly sometimes I feel too overwhelmed to even show up. I don’t know if that counts as affecting things, but… it probably does.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worries keep coming back to you over time, even after you've tried to deal with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I mean, I try to handle it, so it’s not like I’m ignoring things. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I sent the email, it’s done,” but then it still kind of comes back around.\n\nAnd then one little “what if” turns into, like, five more before I can really pull myself out of it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but… yeah, it does keep looping sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I can usually start by telling myself something reasonable, like, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.” But it doesn’t always stick.\n\nIt’s not constant every second or anything, but once it gets going, it can kind of loop. One “what if” turns into a bunch more, and then it takes me a while to pull myself back. I know that probably sounds… excessive, but that’s usually how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s pretty close to every day. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it doesn’t really stay on just one thing anymore.\n\nLike, it used to be mostly the condo stuff, but now it jumps around… whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’m going to mess up on a support call, or how I’m supposed to save money and actually move forward with my life. It’s not always intense the whole day, but it’s there a lot. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still getting by, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s affecting things.

Like I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m worried I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, and then it’s money—like how I’m supposed to save or move forward with my life.\n\nIt sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it’s like my brain keeps finding the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually notice the tension in your body when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always notice it right away, but when I’m worrying, my body feels kind of wound up—like I’m bracing for something bad to happen. Completely stop it? Um… not really, no. I can sometimes calm it down a little, like I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.”

But a lot of the time it doesn’t fully stop. It just keeps looping, and then one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. I know that probably sounds kind of dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.

Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, but not really. I mean, some things are quieter than others, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like there’s a whole area where I just never worry.

But my reaction to them feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike, it doesn’t just stay on the condo anymore. It jumps to, “Is my cousin upset with me?” or “What if I mess up a support call?” or “How am I ever going to save money or move forward?” And each one can feel really important in the moment, even if later I can see maybe it wasn’t as big as it felt.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do or enjoy the things you like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is, more than I’d like to admit. I’m still getting things done, mostly, but I’ve been falling behind on support tickets, and I’ll double-check really simple tasks over and over, which slows me down. A couple coworkers have had to help cover things, which I feel pretty bad about.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped some dance nights and mentoring sessions. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but honestly sometimes I feel too overwhelmed to even show up. I don’t know if that counts as affecting things, but… it probably does.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worries keep coming back to you over time, even after you've tried to deal with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I mean, I try to handle it, so it’s not like I’m ignoring things. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I sent the email, it’s done,” but then it still kind of comes back around.\n\nAnd then one little “what if” turns into, like, five more before I can really pull myself out of it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but… yeah, it does keep looping sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I can usually start by telling myself something reasonable, like, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.” But it doesn’t always stick.\n\nIt’s not constant every second or anything, but once it gets going, it can kind of loop. One “what if” turns into a bunch more, and then it takes me a while to pull myself back. I know that probably sounds… excessive, but that’s usually how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s pretty close to every day. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it doesn’t really stay on just one thing anymore.\n\nLike, it used to be mostly the condo stuff, but now it jumps around… whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’m going to mess up on a support call, or how I’m supposed to save money and actually move forward with my life. It’s not always intense the whole day, but it’s there a lot. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still getting by, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s affecting things.\n\nAt work I’ve been falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking really simple tasks, and sometimes coworkers have had to help cover things I probably should’ve finished myself. That’s… embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped a few dance nights and mentoring sessions, not because I didn’t want to go, but because I felt too overwhelmed to show up. So, yeah, I guess it has been getting in the way of feeling okay overall.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's anything you can do to completely stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Completely stop it? Um… not really, no. I can sometimes calm it down a little, like I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.”\n\nBut a lot of the time it doesn’t fully stop. It just keeps looping, and then one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. I know that probably sounds kind of dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas in your life where you never seem to worry?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, but not really. I mean, some things are quieter than others, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like there’s a whole area where I just never worry.\n\nIt used to feel more contained, like mostly the condo stuff, but now it sort of jumps around. I’ll worry that my cousin is upset with me, then I’ll start thinking about whether I’m going to make a mistake on a support call, and then it turns into money and how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward with my life.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a totally worry-free area. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but… yeah, it does keep looping sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I can usually start by telling myself something reasonable, like, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.” But it doesn’t always stick.\n\nIt’s not constant every second or anything, but once it gets going, it can kind of loop. One “what if” turns into a bunch more, and then it takes me a while to pull myself back. I know that probably sounds… excessive, but that’s usually how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s pretty close to every day. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it doesn’t really stay on just one thing anymore.\n\nLike, it used to be mostly the condo stuff, but now it jumps around… whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’m going to mess up on a support call, or how I’m supposed to save money and actually move forward with my life. It’s not always intense the whole day, but it’s there a lot. That’s… embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped a few dance nights and mentoring sessions, not because I didn’t want to go, but because I felt too overwhelmed to show up. That’s… embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped a few dance nights and mentoring sessions, not because I didn’t want to go, but because I felt too overwhelmed to show up. I can sometimes calm it down a little, like I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.”\n\nBut a lot of the time it doesn’t fully stop. I’m not sure if I’m answering that right, but I wouldn’t describe it as a sudden intense fear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a panic attack, which is a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had what I’d call a panic attack, no. It jumps to, “Is my cousin upset with me?” or “What if I mess up a support call?” or “How am I ever going to save money or move forward?” And each one can feel really important in the moment, even if later I can see maybe it wasn’t as big as it felt.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do or enjoy the things you like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is, more than I’d like to admit. I’m still getting things done, mostly, but I’ve been falling behind on support tickets, and I’ll double-check really simple tasks over and over, which slows me down. A couple coworkers have had to help cover things, which I feel pretty bad about.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped some dance nights and mentoring sessions. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but honestly sometimes I feel too overwhelmed to even show up. I don’t know if that counts as affecting things, but… it probably does.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worries keep coming back to you over time, even after you've tried to deal with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I mean, I try to handle it, so it’s not like I’m ignoring things. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I sent the email, it’s done,” but then it still kind of comes back around.\n\nAnd then one little “what if” turns into, like, five more before I can really pull myself out of it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but… yeah, it does keep looping sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I can usually start by telling myself something reasonable, like, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.” But it doesn’t always stick.\n\nIt’s not constant every second or anything, but once it gets going, it can kind of loop. One “what if” turns into a bunch more, and then it takes me a while to pull myself back. I know that probably sounds… excessive, but that’s usually how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s pretty close to every day. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it doesn’t really stay on just one thing anymore.\n\nLike, it used to be mostly the condo stuff, but now it jumps around… whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’m going to mess up on a support call, or how I’m supposed to save money and actually move forward with my life. It’s not always intense the whole day, but it’s there a lot. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still getting by, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s affecting things.\n\nAt work I’ve been falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking really simple tasks, and sometimes coworkers have had to help cover things I probably should’ve finished myself. That’s… embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped a few dance nights and mentoring sessions, not because I didn’t want to go, but because I felt too overwhelmed to show up. So, yeah, I guess it has been getting in the way of feeling okay overall.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's anything you can do to completely stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Completely stop it? Um… not really, no. I can sometimes calm it down a little, like I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.”\n\nBut a lot of the time it doesn’t fully stop. It just keeps looping, and then one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. I know that probably sounds kind of dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas in your life where you never seem to worry?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, but not really. I mean, some things are quieter than others, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like there’s a whole area where I just never worry.\n\nIt used to feel more contained, like mostly the condo stuff, but now it sort of jumps around. I’ll worry that my cousin is upset with me, then I’ll start thinking about whether I’m going to make a mistake on a support call, and then it turns into money and how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward with my life.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a totally worry-free area. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I do feel kind of wound up a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something bad to happen, even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to enjoy something like dance night. I can look normal on the outside, I think, but inside I feel on edge. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like it comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast within minutes.\n\nI mean, I do get anxious and worked up, but it feels more like it builds or keeps running in the background, if that makes sense. I’m not sure if I’m answering that right, but I wouldn’t describe it as a sudden intense fear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a panic attack, which is a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had what I’d call a panic attack, no. I mean, I get really anxious sometimes, but not that sudden, overwhelming wave where it peaks within minutes.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what counts as one… but from how you’re describing it, I don’t think that’s happened to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are usually about things that are likely to happen?\n\nPatient: Probably not always, no. I mean… some of them could happen, I guess, but I know I take it too far.\n\nIt’s not even just the condo anymore. My mind jumps around—like, is my cousin upset with me, am I going to mess up on a support call, how am I ever going to save enough money or move forward with my life. I can usually make it sound reasonable in my head, but saying it out loud, it does feel like I’m worrying about a lot of things that may not actually happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are excessive and persistent, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying that, because I know some worries are normal, but mine do feel like they keep going even when there isn’t really a clear reason.\n\nIt doesn’t stay on one thing, either. It’ll start with the condo, and then suddenly I’m worrying about whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save enough money or move forward. It’s like my brain keeps finding something else to attach to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced repeated episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I’ve had moments where I feel really stressed or keyed up, but not repeated episodes where it suddenly gets intensely scary and peaks within a few minutes. Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it has started getting in the way.

At work I’ve been falling behind on support tickets, and I catch myself double-checking really basic tasks over and over. A couple coworkers have had to pick up some slack, which I feel pretty embarrassed about.

And socially, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions—not because I didn’t want to go, exactly, but because I felt too overwhelmed to show up. So… yes, I guess it is affecting my daily life and relationships more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of interference you mean?
=====================================

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling really wound up most of the time. It’s probably not, like, dramatic or anything, but my body feels tense a lot—almost like I’m bracing for something bad to happen, even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax and enjoy dance night. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you think is causing you to feel this tension?\n\nPatient: I mean… there isn’t just one thing, I don’t think. It used to be more about my condo stuff, but now it kind of jumps around. Like I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m worried I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, and then it’s money—like how I’m supposed to save or move forward with my life.\n\nIt sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it’s like my brain keeps finding the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually notice the tension in your body when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always notice it right away, but when I’m worrying, my body feels kind of wound up—like I’m bracing for something bad to happen. Even if I’m just at my desk or trying to have a normal night out dancing, there’s this tension there. I don’t know if that’s the “right” way to describe it, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a wide range of things, or are they mostly focused on a few specific areas?\n\nPatient: It’s… more of a range, I think. It used to feel mostly tied to the condo, so that was easier to explain, but now it doesn’t really stay there. It’ll jump to, like, whether my cousin is upset with me, or if I’m going to make some mistake on a support call, or money—like how I’m supposed to save and move forward.\n\nI know those are kind of normal things to think about, probably, but it feels like my mind just keeps picking a new one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are bigger than the problems themselves?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like my problems are huge or anything, because they’re pretty normal things, I think. But my reaction to them feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike, it doesn’t just stay on the condo anymore. It jumps to, “Is my cousin upset with me?” or “What if I mess up a support call?” or “How am I ever going to save money or move forward?” And each one can feel really important in the moment, even if later I can see maybe it wasn’t as big as it felt.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do or enjoy the things you like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is, more than I’d like to admit. I’m still getting things done, mostly, but I’ve been falling behind on support tickets, and I’ll double-check really simple tasks over and over, which slows me down. A couple coworkers have had to help cover things, which I feel pretty bad about.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped some dance nights and mentoring sessions. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but honestly sometimes I feel too overwhelmed to even show up. I don’t know if that counts as affecting things, but… it probably does.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worries keep coming back to you over time, even after you've tried to deal with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I mean, I try to handle it, so it’s not like I’m ignoring things. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I sent the email, it’s done,” but then it still kind of comes back around.\n\nAnd then one little “what if” turns into, like, five more before I can really pull myself out of it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but… yeah, it does keep looping sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I can usually start by telling myself something reasonable, like, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.” But it doesn’t always stick.\n\nIt’s not constant every second or anything, but once it gets going, it can kind of loop. One “what if” turns into a bunch more, and then it takes me a while to pull myself back. I know that probably sounds… excessive, but that’s usually how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s pretty close to every day. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it doesn’t really stay on just one thing anymore.\n\nLike, it used to be mostly the condo stuff, but now it jumps around… whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’m going to mess up on a support call, or how I’m supposed to save money and actually move forward with my life. It’s not always intense the whole day, but it’s there a lot. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still getting by, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s affecting things.\n\nAt work I’ve been falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking really simple tasks, and sometimes coworkers have had to help cover things I probably should’ve finished myself. That’s… embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped a few dance nights and mentoring sessions, not because I didn’t want to go, but because I felt too overwhelmed to show up. So, yeah, I guess it has been getting in the way of feeling okay overall.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's anything you can do to completely stop worrying once you've started?\n\nPatient: Completely stop it? Um… not really, no. I can sometimes calm it down a little, like I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You sent the email, it’s done.”\n\nBut a lot of the time it doesn’t fully stop. It just keeps looping, and then one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. I know that probably sounds kind of dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas in your life where you never seem to worry?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, but not really. I mean, some things are quieter than others, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like there’s a whole area where I just never worry.\n\nIt used to feel more contained, like mostly the condo stuff, but now it sort of jumps around. I’ll worry that my cousin is upset with me, then I’ll start thinking about whether I’m going to make a mistake on a support call, and then it turns into money and how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward with my life.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a totally worry-free area. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I do feel kind of wound up a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something bad to happen, even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to enjoy something like dance night. I can look normal on the outside, I think, but inside I feel on edge. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like it comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast within minutes.\n\nI mean, I do get anxious and worked up, but it feels more like it builds or keeps running in the background, if that makes sense. I’m not sure if I’m answering that right, but I wouldn’t describe it as a sudden intense fear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a panic attack, which is a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had what I’d call a panic attack, no. I mean, I get really anxious sometimes, but not that sudden, overwhelming wave where it peaks within minutes.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what counts as one… but from how you’re describing it, I don’t think that’s happened to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are usually about things that are likely to happen?\n\nPatient: Probably not always, no. I mean… some of them could happen, I guess, but I know I take it too far.\n\nIt’s not even just the condo anymore. My mind jumps around—like, is my cousin upset with me, am I going to mess up on a support call, how am I ever going to save enough money or move forward with my life. I can usually make it sound reasonable in my head, but saying it out loud, it does feel like I’m worrying about a lot of things that may not actually happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are excessive and persistent, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love saying that, because I know some worries are normal, but mine do feel like they keep going even when there isn’t really a clear reason.\n\nIt doesn’t stay on one thing, either. It’ll start with the condo, and then suddenly I’m worrying about whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save enough money or move forward. It’s like my brain keeps finding something else to attach to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced repeated episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I’ve had moments where I feel really stressed or keyed up, but not repeated episodes where it suddenly gets intensely scary and peaks within a few minutes. At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it has started getting in the way.\n\nAt work I’ve been falling behind on support tickets, and I catch myself double-checking really basic tasks over and over. So… yes, I guess it is affecting my daily life and relationships more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
